Abstract: A coarse description of a dynamical system generally fails to evolve autonomously: evolving and coarse-graining in different orders gives different predictions, and the compressed propagator fails the semigroup law. We show that this path dependence is the observable boundary of missing state. For a finite continuous-time Markov chain with generator $L$, coarse channel $C$, and stochastic section $R$ satisfying $CR=I$, define $P=RC$, $Q=I-P$, and $K=CLR$. The exact coarse propagator $T_t=C\mathrm{e}^{tL}R$ obeys a Volterra equation with memory kernel $\mathcal{M}(t)=CLQ\,\mathrm{e}^{tQLQ}\,QLR$. Every memory jet $\mathcal{M}^{(k)}(0)$ is recursively recoverable from the short-time derivatives of $T_t$ alone. The rank of the block Hankel operator $\mathbb{H}_{\infty}=(\mathcal{M}^{(i+j)}(0))_{i,j\geq 0}$ is exactly the smallest number of auxiliary linear state variables required to turn the coarse dynamics into an exact autonomous, time-homogeneous system. A minimal completion is constructible by Ho--Kalman factorization and is unique up to similarity. If $d=\min\{k:\mathcal{M}^{(k)}(0)\neq 0\}$, then every smooth classical $f$-divergence generically scales as $t^{2d+4}$. Exact Markov examples realize exponents $4,6,8$ and closure ranks $1,2,3$. We also derive finite-sample singular-value certificates for hidden predictive coordinates, including a matrix-Bernstein bound with intrinsic-variance control. Finally, histories are identified when they induce the same future law under every admissible intervention, yielding the minimal counterfactual predictive state. The results turn coarse-graining into a constructive state-completion problem and separate ordinary linear realization from the harder requirement of a positive or Markov realization.

</details>

Abstract: We present the concept of quadrature-detected zero-to-ultralow-field nuclear magnetic resonance (QD ZULF NMR). Two orthogonal sensors, atomic optically pumped magnetometers, are used to separately detect components of oscillating sample magnetization, which are then combined into a quadrature signal. This approach enables the distinction of positive and negative resonance frequencies with respect to the laboratory frame---such that absolute signs of gyromagnetic ratios can be determined. In addition, QD ZULF NMR reduces peak density and overlap compared to ZULF NMR with single-axis detection, while providing richer information about nuclear-spin dynamics. As an illustration, we present experimental $J$-spectra for thermally polarized [$^{13}$C]-formic acid and [$^{13}$C]-methanol, compounds commonly used in ZULF NMR which represent XA and XA$_3$ spin systems, respectively. A theoretical framework describing QD ZULF NMR signals is provided.

</details>

Abstract: Long-baseline optical links for space interferometry and satellite quantum networks rely on heterodyne phase tracking under Doppler motion and gravitational frequency shifts. These relativistic shifts can generate large detector-frame beat-frequency offsets over finite readout windows and thereby constrain the temporal precision of front-end phase readout. We develop a detector-frame framework for this problem using an effective two-mode signal--local-oscillator model in which relativistic motion rescales the local-oscillator frequency. Within this setting we derive closed-form Mandelstam--Tamm and Margolus--Levitin bounds for representative Gaussian resources and connect global state distinguishability with the operational precision of heterodyne phase tracking. In the bright-local-oscillator regime we identify a geometric--operational mismatch: the joint optical state can evolve on arbitrarily short global timescales while the relevant information rate is set by the measured detector record. Combining this result with an open-loop transient-acquisition analysis, we show that white phase noise and relativistic beat-frequency drift that is approximately constant over the acquisition window generate a cube-root scaling benchmark for the optimal pre-lock open-loop integration window and the minimum residual phase error. These results establish detector-frame benchmarks for quantum-limited heterodyne timing in relativistic optical links and clarify how global dynamical bounds enter task-restricted continuous optical measurements.

</details>

Abstract: Weak measurement has proven useful for metrology, for probing quantum paradoxes, and for the direct measurement of quantum states. Weak measurements of polarization qubits with temporal pointers provide a direct route to weak-value characterization, including in regimes where the pointer is only partially coherent. We derive a closed-form expression for the post-selected temporal shift that interpolates between the incoherent and coherent (ideal) weak-measurement limits through a single parameter $ν$, the pointer visibility. The expression is experimentally tested for a range of polarization states spanning three trajectories corresponding to rotations about the principal axes of the Poincaré sphere. Two of the trajectories provide parameter-free benchmarks, with the real part of the weak value following $\cos^2θ$ and $1/2$. In contrast, the weak value along the third trajectory is sensitive to incoherence and is well described by the single-parameter partial-coherence model, from which a reduced pointer visibility is obtained. Time shifts are extracted from the smoothed derivative of the pulse leading edge with internal calibration, reaching few-picosecond precision on 10 ns optical pulses using commercial components. The same apparatus also gives qualitative access to the imaginary quadrature through the frequency centroid, showing that temporal weak measurements remain accessible and quantitative in partially coherent pulsed systems.

Abstract: In this work, we study a theoretical scheme to investigate the monogamy of quantum steering in a hybrid exciton--cavity optomechanical (EOM) system. Within the Gaussian-state formalism, we quantify the steerability among the exciton, photon, and mechanical modes in both the steady-state and dynamical regimes. We establish and verify CKW-type monogamy inequalities for Gaussian steering and demonstrate that the distribution of bipartite steering among the three modes can be controlled through the system parameters. We further examine the influence of thermal noise and temporal evolution on the steering properties and the corresponding monogamy relations. Our results show that increasing the temperature generally suppresses the strength of Gaussian steering and modifies the degree of steering sharing imposed by the CKW-type monogamy relations. In contrast, in the dynamical regime, the steerability can increase with evolution time over suitable temporal windows, indicating that the transient dynamics can be exploited to enhance quantum correlations. These results highlight the potential of hybrid exciton--optomechanical platforms for controllable quantum correlations and provide a promising route toward the generation and manipulation of multipartite quantum resources.

</details>

Abstract: Quantum machine learning (QML) is a promising application of quantum computing, yet when and how practical learning models can achieve quantum advantage remains poorly understood. Even a polynomial improvement in the resources needed to attain a given predictive performance would be significant. In this work, we investigate empirical scaling laws of quantum vision transformers (QViTs), which connect QML to the transformer architecture underlying modern large language models. We ask whether QViTs exhibit scaling behavior analogous to classical models and how this behavior changes when qubit number is included alongside dataset size and model parameters. The validation losses in our numerical image-classification experiments are well described by a joint shifted power-law model in qubit number, transformer depth, and training dataset size. Expressing this fit in terms of the classical encoder parameter budget identifies a regime in which increasing the number of qubits can compensate for fewer classical encoder parameters at comparable predicted performance. This finding is consistent with earlier observations of parameter efficiency in quantum learning models and suggests a direction for exploring possible resource savings in QML.

</details>

Abstract: Stationary scattering admits a dynamical formulation in which the scattering data are extracted from the evolution operator for an effective non-stationary and non-unitary quantum system. This provides a mathematical method of solving scattering problems. We consider the possibility of realizing such a quantum system and relating the scattering information to outcomes of quantum measurements performed on it. To this end, we examine stationary scattering problems in one dimension where the scattering data consist of reflection and transmission amplitudes and the corresponding effective quantum system is a non-unitary two-level system. The states of this system identify points in a Bloch sphere $S^2$ and their evolution yields trajectories in $S^2$. Quantum measurements turn out to be capable of providing information about the reflection amplitudes. We identify configurations displaying reflectionlessness with situations where one or both of the poles of $S^2$ undergo cyclic evolutions. Spectral singularities and coherent perfect absorption correspond to dynamical spin flips which map one of the poles of $S^2$ to the other.

Abstract: Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) uses fixed quantum dynamics as a high-dimensional feature map. Still, standard pipelines flatten reservoir measurements and train a linear readout, ignoring the natural tensor structure across time steps, qubits, and observables. We propose a multilinear readout for quantum reservoir computing (ML-QRC), a tensor-structured neural readout compressed through mode-wise projections. Relative to a dense hidden-width-64 neural readout, this reduces the feature-to-hidden parameter count from $64TnF$ to $d(T+n+F)+64d^3$, giving a $12.1\times$ feature-block compression at $T=50$, $n=6$, $d=8$, and $F=n+\binom{n}{2}$. Across Mackey-Glass forecasting and TFIM phase classification, ML-QRC remains statistically competitive with Ridge on Mackey-Glass and obtains the numerically highest full-data TFIM accuracy among tested methods, while using $12.0\times$ fewer parameters than the full dense MLP. Its clearest benefit is improved low-data stability relative to neural MLP readouts on Mackey-Glass; this advantage does not transfer to TFIM, where Ridge performs best at 50\% data. Overall, ML-QRC is best viewed as a tensor-structured neural readout that compresses dense MLP readouts while preserving QRC tensor structure, rather than as a replacement for simple linear/Ridge readouts in the tested regime.

</details>

Abstract: In molecules, electronic coherence typically decays within a few hundred femtoseconds, a timescale usually attributed to the environment. We show that it is set to a large extent by which coherences are excited: the molecule can host coherences that the bath barely sees, and optimal control can find and exploit them. Using gradient-based optimal control (GRAPE) with Redfield dynamics, we maximize the total vibronic coherence between the electronic ground and an optically bright electronic excited state in a three-state model with nonadiabatic features, in two regimes of low and high nonadiabatic mixing and for different system-bath coupling strengths. The Liouvillian spectrum reveals, in both regimes, a well-separated coherence mode with substantial ground-bright character that decays on a picosecond timescale. This mode is protected by mixing with the dark state, which brings the geometry of the optically accessible excited state close to that of the ground state and thus weakens its coupling to the bath. Without prior information, GRAPE finds this mode and keeps the photoexcited wave packet near the Franck-Condon region. At equal fluence, it increases the coherence at 800 fs by more than two orders of magnitude over a Gaussian pulse at the weakest coupling, and by many more at stronger coupling. The Liouvillian predicts the bath dependence of the long-lived coherence to within 1\%, and a Gaussian pulse tuned to the mode frequency comes within 10\% of the best pulse found. Beyond two-level and spin systems, our study puts forward the vibronic structure of molecules as a route to protected coherence subspaces, identifying a dark-state protection mechanism and a design criterion based on molecular parameters. These results lay the groundwork for our goal of using vibronic molecular systems as a resource for long-lived, controllable coherences in molecular quantum information science.

</details>

Abstract: Small particles respond to light through electric and magnetic patterns called multipoles. Their interference determines the direction of scattered light and affects the decay of nearby emitters. Larger nanoantenna arrays, metasurfaces, and other nanophotonic systems demand increasingly costly simulations to meet strict performance and reliability requirements. We show how to represent these responses with quantum bits (qubits) and use quantum circuits to calculate scattering and the Purcell effect, the change in an emitter's decay rate caused by its surroundings. The circuits can encode known multipoles or estimate unknown ones from Maxwell's equations at the particle surface. We test the method against classical theory using an ideal quantum simulator and real IBM and IonQ quantum computers. For a silicon nanoparticle of radius 78 nm illuminated at 679 nm, IonQ gives a forward directivity of 2.984, close to the ideal dipolar value 3, quantifying the concentration of scattered light in the forward direction. For an emitter 10 nm from a gold nanoparticle and emitting at 510 nm, IBM measurements yield a calculated total decay rate 20.48 times its vacuum value versus 20.47 from theory, with correction for errors in reading the qubit states. We also establish how the number of multipoles and the required accuracy set the demands on qubits and measurements. These results motivate quantum-assisted design studies of directional radiation and emitter decay in nanophotonic systems.

</details>

Abstract: Hybrid quantum neural networks (QNNs) pair a classical feature extractor with a variational quantum circuit, and the effective dimension of the circuit's quantum Fisher information matrix (QFIM) is widely used to assess such models. This scalar is often interpreted as an indicator of trainability or accuracy, although these readings are rarely tested under controlled conditions. We therefore investigate what it actually measures. We define the effective dimension as the participation ratio of the QFIM diagonal, which lies between one and the parameter count, and we evaluate it across two image datasets, four frozen backbones including a random-projection control, four circuit depths on eight qubits, and three seeds. During training, the effective dimension remains well below the parameter count, and its correlation with test accuracy ranges from -0.72 to +0.87, changing sign across seeds in half of the configurations. Increasing depth activates a larger share of the circuit directions, from 0.38 to 0.91 of the parameter count, while the initialization gradient variance remains between 1e-5 and 1e-4, so no barren plateau (BP) appears at eight qubits. At a fixed circuit, the four backbones share an initial effective dimension of 6 to 9, even though CIFAR-10 accuracy ranges from 23.14% to 86.44%. These results show that the effective dimension characterizes circuit geometry rather than model quality. We conclude with a practical protocol that assigns each diagnostic to the question it can answer.

</details>

Abstract: Quantum random number generators are crucial for information security, yet existing implementations are hindered by bulky optics, device imperfections, and state alignment constraints. Here, using a compact metasurface platform, we achieve polarization-independent certified randomness by establishing a quantitative quantum graph framework. By mapping multi-channel nanophotonic responses onto graph vertices, we assign metasurface measurements an operational information-theoretic meaning, directly connecting weighted graph degrees to conditional min-entropy estimation. The resulting graph-certified measurements yield robust private randomness even under entanglement-based attacks without complex optics. Experimentally, we fabricate 4- and 6-channel dielectric metasurfaces to implement high-dimensional positive operator-valued measure graphs, successfully certifying randomness with heralded single photons and Poissonian light. By integrating complex quantum measurements into a single passive layer, our metasurface quantum graphs provide a scalable route for integrated quantum technologies.

Abstract: Exact permutation covariance does not itself supply a sparse metric geometry. For permutation-covariant, full-support $k$-body interaction orbits, the incident interaction norm scales as $\binom{N-1}{k-1}|J_N|\|h\|$; such orbits cannot have uniformly finite range in metrics of diverging diameter. A permutation-invariant Hamiltonian diagonal in the graph basis has ground-state multiplicity at least $N!/|\mathrm{Aut}(G)|$ whenever $G$ is a ground graph. We give a quantitative version for approximately symmetric energies, together with fixed-radius connectivity and unitary-isometry obstructions. The trace-distance asymmetry of an arbitrary state also bounds its probability of selecting one labeled graph.
  We then construct two exactly solvable finite relational history Hamiltonians. A cyclic clock with a reversible incidence update has a unique ground state; gauge reduction yields a cycle Laplacian with a rank-one pin and the exact gap $Δ_{N,L}=E_* (1-\cosθ_L)$, where $\tan(Lθ_L/2)=1/\sinθ_L$ and $0<θ_L<π/L$. In particular, $Δ_{N,L}/E_*=π^2/(2L^2)-2π^2/L^3+O(L^{-4})$, independently of the number of vertices. In an alternative order-free architecture, one clock qubit per unordered pair gives commuting four-local terms, $2^N$ ground states and a size-independent gap $E_*(1-1/\sqrt2)$. A fixed-weight control penalty selects $\binom Nq$ ground states without reducing the gap. These exact finite results expose clock and locality resource tradeoffs; they do not establish an emergent geometric phase.

</details>

Abstract: Quantum aging transition has recently been reported, whereby the oscillatory dynamics of a network of globally coupled quantum self-sustained oscillators is degraded sharply when the fraction of inactive nodes exceeds a critical value. Here we explore the reverse process: the revival of rhythmic dynamics from an aged state in a quantum oscillator network by introducing a controlled incoherent single-boson pumping channel. Using a self-consistent quantum master equation in the Lindblad form for globally coupled active--inactive quantum Stuart--Landau oscillators, we show that increasing the pumping strength progressively suppresses the aging transition and eventually eliminates it, restoring rhythmicity even when all oscillators are inactive. Remarkably, complete compensation of the coupling-induced dissipative loss is not required; a substantially weaker pumping strength is sufficient to eliminate the aging transition. We show that the revival is governed by a redistribution of the Fock-state population of the inactive oscillators toward higher-occupation states. Our results establish controlled incoherent pumping as an effective mechanism for restoring dynamical robustness in quantum oscillator networks.

</details>

Abstract: A photon has the strange property that it travels along a null interval with zero proper time, hence its emission and absorption points are, in a sense, a singular event. For their part, the emission and absorption processes have a finite duration which determines the spectral content of the photon. The question in the title points to the conceptual difficulty in `stitching' together these three intervals, which all correspond to the same physical entity but have fundamentally incompatible temporal descriptions. A previously developed model for quantum wavefunction propagation solves this apparent dilemma by formulating the life cycle of a photon in terms of recursive Fourier transforms on dual spaces. Because it doesn't treat space as dependent on time, and it is built on the Fourier transform whose phase is an invariant in special relativity as well as the foundation of the uncertainty principle, the model is a promising candidate framework for making quantum mechanics compatible with relativity. The model proposes to serve as an improved Klein-Gordon equation, or relativistically compatible replacement for Schrödinger's equation.

</details>

Abstract: Approximate quantum compilation (AQC) using tensor networks provides a promising route to reducing the circuit resources required for quantum simulation. However, the factors governing the compressibility of many-body dynamics and the extent to which compressed circuits retain physically relevant information on quantum hardware remain poorly understood. We investigate these questions using AQCtensor for an 80-qubit one-dimensional anisotropic XYZ Heisenberg chain initialized in a Néel state. By varying the exchange anisotropy and external magnetic field, we find that compression performance changes systematically with the anisotropic coupling configuration and field orientation. These variations are strongly associated with a nested-commutator measure related to second-order Trotter error, with corresponding directional trends also reflected in the target-state MPS bond dimension. For an 80-qubit quantum-hardware evaluation, AQCtensor compresses a 10-repetition target evolution to 5 repetitions while retaining a target-state fidelity of 0.995474, reducing the total circuit depth by 45.9% and the two-qubit gate count by 47.6%. The compressed circuit exhibits comparable behavior across magnetization, nearest-neighbor correlation, and Hamiltonian expectation measurements, while process-level diagnostics and local reduced-state tomography provide complementary characterization beyond these observables. Together, these results link AQCtensor compressibility to Hamiltonian commutator structure and target-state complexity and demonstrate substantial circuit reduction while retaining physically relevant features in an 80-qubit quantum-hardware experiment.

</details>

Abstract: Weak measurements are used to amplify small signals, to address foundational questions, and to characterise quantum states without tomography. In direct state reconstruction each amplitude of the state vector is proportional to a single complex weak value, which for a temporal pointer is read from the mean arrival time and the mean frequency of the postselected pulse. Such an experiment requires three quantities to be fixed, the coupling, the angular resolution demanded of the reconstruction, and the set of states over which the method remains usable, and these are commonly set by the available apparatus rather than derived. We derive all three for a Gaussian temporal pointer and an arbitrary postselection state. The states that cannot be reconstructed form a spherical cap centred on the state orthogonal to the postselection, whose geodesic half-angle follows from the largest of three independent thresholds and involves no adjustable parameter. Two states are distinguishable only if they differ by more than a minimal step fixed by the coupling, by the number of incident photons and by the sensitivity of the weak value along the path followed. For an inversion at first order in the coupling the systematic and statistical errors are opposed and fix an optimal coupling that varies as the inverse sixth root of the number of incident photons, whereas an exact inversion at finite coupling removes the systematic error, the remaining bound being one on the two-centroid readout rather than on the inversion, since below an amplitude equal to a quarter of the coupling two states give the same pair of centroids while their profiles differ. These results allow a direct reconstruction experiment to be dimensioned before the apparatus is assembled.

Abstract: A variational quantum state may have many control angles, and its complete gradient contains one derivative for each. The task is to turn access to selected derivatives into a shared finite-shot record for the complete vector. The Hopf ansatz supplies the setting: it constructs any normalized pure state from nested two-way magnitude splits and, for complex states, leaf phases, with one directly programmed angle per split or phase. Earlier Hopf constructions prepare the state, recover coordinates from known amplitudes, and provide indexed derivative access. With a phase-calibrated controlled reflection, the inverse Hopf frame maps each normalized magnitude direction to a computational label, so every all-$X$ outcome contributes to every magnitude derivative. A separate signed leaf record supplies the complex phase block. A checkpoint variant reverses only the suffix below a selected depth and returns that layer's gradient block. For a complete chart with $M$ real-valued parameters, sufficient execution counts are $\mathcal{O}(\log\log M)$ for the global method and $\mathcal{O}(\log M\log\log M)$ for the complete checkpoint schedule at fixed additive coordinate accuracy and confidence. Abstract: Entangled few-photon states, such as Bell and Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) states, are central resources for discrete-variable photonic quantum information processing. In many architectures, these states are generated probabilistically by first heralding single photons from biphoton sources and then interfering them in linear-optical circuits. In this work, we show that the entanglement structure of said biphoton sources can be directly exploited for noise-engineering to deliver higher quality resource states. Using the transpose identity of multi-mode squeezed vacuum states, we introduce "ghost-unitary" implementations in which the target resource-generation interferometer is applied to the heralding modes rather than to the delivered resource modes. When the dominant imperfection is interferometric loss, this relocation converts direct amplitude damping of the output into false-positive heralding events that require both higher-pair emission and photon loss. In the low-squeezing regime, these events are parametrically suppressed, yielding higher-fidelity conditional resource states. We analyze this mechanism for single-qubit states, two-photon NOON states, Bell states, and a family of $n$-qubit GHZ states, as well as for single-qubit teleportation and type-I-fusion-based resource growth. Across these examples, we delineate finite parameter regimes in which ghost-unitary implementations outperform direct implementations, with particular relevance to frequency-bin photonics where active linear-optical transformations can be relatively lossy. We also prove that, in the setting considered here, the direct implementation of the $n$-GHZ family delivers identically damped outputs: equivalent to the ideal state followed by uniform amplitude damping. This extends the known $n=2,3$ cases and provides a structural result of independent interest for photonic quantum information processing.

Abstract: Quantum signal processing (QSP) requires the efficient computation of phase factors associated with a polynomial transformation. One existing stable factorization method avoids direct root finding but relies on Prony's method to recover a polynomial from weighted power sums of its roots (arXiv:2202.02671). We present a modification of this approach that eliminates Prony's method by using the logarithmic derivative of an associated polynomial. Because the logarithmic derivative has the same residue at every root after accounting for multiplicity, its Fourier coefficients directly yield the unweighted power sums of the roots inside the unit disk. The Newton--Girard identities then recover the coefficients of the corresponding polynomial without computing its roots. The resulting algorithm has $O(d\log^2 d)$ computational complexity and simplifies the factorization procedure of the original method. In numerical experiments on Hamiltonian simulation, eigenstate filtering, matrix inversion, and the Fermi--Dirac operator, our implementation computes phase factors in seconds with maximum errors on the order of $10^{-12}$.

Abstract: A Dirac mass is characterized not only by its magnitude but also by its sign, which influences the Berry curvature, Hall response, and the propagation direction of chiral boundary modes. These results pave the way for imaging the locations where the topological character changes within a material, rather than merely identifying where a spectral gap appears.

Abstract: Charger-mediated quantum batteries offer promising opportunities for efficient energy transfer and storage at the quantum level, with potential advantages beyond their classical counterparts. Here, we theoretically demonstrate that nonlinear charger-battery interactions can substantially accelerate the charging of quantum batteries. In the weak-driving regime, sufficiently strong nonlinear coupling can dominate over the effect of dephasing and significantly reduce the charging time. In the strong- and optimal-driving regimes, where the optimal driving strength maximizes the steady-state ergotropy in the linear-coupling regime, nonlinear coupling gives rise to a new optimum in the charging time beyond that of the linear-coupling regime, accompanied by nearly critically damped dynamics. Remarkably, even in the absence of external driving, nonlinear coupling alone can substantially reduce the charging time, providing an alternative route toward fast battery charging. Our results establish nonlinear charger-battery coupling as an effective resource for accelerating quantum-battery charging and broaden the scope of interaction-enabled quantum advantages in energy transfer and storage.

Abstract: Sensing time-varying signals is essential for precision measurement and for characterizing fluctuations in physical systems. In this work, we show that quantum entanglement provides an exponential advantage in the acquisition time required to learn the temporal statistics of a stationary classical signal. The task is to estimate the characteristic function of joint displacements on selected bosonic modes within a fixed-duration window, using continuous measurements of one signal realization. These results establish an exponential quantum learning advantage for a single continuously monitored stationary signal, with the sensing cost measured by total acquisition time.

Abstract: We investigate dynamical quantum phase transitions (DQPTs) generated by a two-step linear quench with a finite waiting time at the equilibrium critical point. The waiting stage leads to the accumulation of a dynamical phase that depends on the waiting time and directly reflects the critical spectral structure. In the transverse Ising chain, this accumulated phase modifies the critical structure of DQPTs from a single-critical-mode (SCM) regime to a multiple-critical-mode (MCM) regime. We analytically and numerically determine the boundary between the two regimes. Along this boundary, the waiting time approaches a constant in the sudden-quench limit, whereas in the slow-quench limit it follows Kibble--Zurek (KZ) scaling with the quench time. In the SCM regime, the rate function exhibits a conventional linear singularity, $r(t)-r(t^{*})\propto |t-t^{*}|^α$, with the critical exponent $α=1$ near the critical time. At the SCM--MCM boundary, this exponent changes to $α=1/2$, leading to a divergent first derivative of the rate function. Additional discrete parameter lines with $α=1/2$ also appear within the MCM regime in the parameter plane spanned by the waiting and quench times. Our findings establish a direct connection between the accumulated dynamical phase and the critical structure of DQPTs, further supported by an extended Su--Schrieffer--Heeger (SSH) model.

</details>

We experimentally validate this approach on IBM quantum hardware by identifying physical defects and implementing the defect-avoidance circuits. As a result, avoiding the defect yields an approximately 10\% reduction in the logical error rate per cycle for both logical $X$ and $Z$ errors. These findings demonstrate that defects in superconducting hardware can be successfully handled even under the constrained connectivity of heavy-hexagon lattices, and that defect avoidance substantially reduces logical errors on real quantum hardware.

Abstract: Area laws express the expectation that entanglement in gapped quantum ground states scales with a boundary rather than a volume. In one dimension this holds for every Rényi entropy, and the Rényi area-law conjecture asserts the same in higher dimensions for each fixed $0<α<1$. Here we disprove this conjecture in every spatial dimension two and higher. We construct finite-range Hamiltonians with fixed local dimension, bounded interaction strength, unique ground states and a uniform spectral gap, whose ground states exhibit volume-law Rényi entanglement while obeying a von Neumann area law and remaining connected to product states through uniformly gapped local paths. The mechanism is error-space complexity: locally identifiable components of arbitrarily small weight can encode states with high entanglement complexity. We also establish the worst-case rank-error trade-off for approximate ground-state projectors, a key tool in one-dimensional area-law proofs. At fixed gap, the optimal Schmidt rank at projection error $ε$ across a planar cut of cross-section $A$ scales as $\exp[\tildeΘ((A\logε^{-1})^{2/3})]$ when $\logε^{-1}\gtrsim A$. Thus $α=1$ is the sharp threshold for a universal Rényi area law within this adiabatically connected class.

Abstract: Quantum computation offers a promising alternative to classical Monte Carlo methods by naturally avoiding sign problems through unitary evolution. A common misconception follows from this: because quantum simulation sidesteps the sign problem, it is often assumed to be an essentially perfect substitute for classical sampling. This is false---quantum measurements are intrinsically stochastic, and estimating observables often requires a number of samples that grows exponentially with system size, time separation, or inverse temperature, exactly as in the classical signal-to-noise problem. In this work, we analyze the origin and structure of signal-to-noise problems in quantum computation, drawing parallels with classical Monte Carlo, and demonstrate that such noise persists even in sign-problem-free regimes. We discuss strategies to mitigate it, in particular a quantum generalization of Stein control variates construction, in which unbiased estimators are built from operator commutators $[X,A]$ and their discrete diagonal counterparts, and validate the approach with exactly solvable numerical examples that quantify the achievable variance reduction. Our results underscore that variance reduction, not merely sign-problem avoidance, is required to make quantum algorithms practically viable for many-body physics.

Abstract: The Gorini-Kossakowski-Lindblad-Sudarshan (GKLS) equation, a cornerstone of open-system theory, describes Markovian semigroup dynamics in which information about the initial preparation cannot revive. Beyond this regime, information revivals are widely interpreted as backflow from a non-Markovian environment. However, a decrease followed by an increase in information retrievable from the system need not imply that information has returned from the environment [PRX Quantum 6, 020316 (2025)]. It may instead arise from noise correlations unrelated to the system's initial preparation, carried by inert degrees of freedom that never interact with the system and therefore cannot mediate backflow. Such revivals are called extrinsic; genuine backflow is reserved for revivals that cannot be explained this way. The framework developed in that work, however, assumes access to arbitrary initial system states, an assumption often incompatible with physical constraints or limited experimental control. This leaves open which properties of the available preparations are necessary for an information revival to certify genuine backflow. Here we extend the framework to allow the description of restricted families of initial states and show that genuine backflow is possible only if the family contains noncommuting states. If all available states commute, as in a classical stochastic process, every revival can be explained by a suitable inert extension and is therefore extrinsic. Noncommutativity of the available system states is thus necessary for information revivals to provide an operational signature of environmental memory.

</details>

It also rules out exact leader election with termination in finite time with probability one on these families. Measurements, classical control, and local memory growing with time are allowed; no predetermined running-time bound is assumed. Quantum interference thus defeats execution lifting without enabling infallible certification of network size.

The first is the rate at which squeezing is generated: the exponential rate follows in closed form, vanishes exactly when the two strengths are equal in magnitude, and recovers as the square root of the detuning from that point. This paper reports an end-to-end fidelity and rate analysis for islands-based ZALM that includes: SPDC multipair emissions of all orders and partial-BSM losses in the ZALM QTX; loss in propagation from the ZALM QTX to Alice and Bob's quantum receivers (QRXs); loss and mode mismatch in the mode converters employed at Alice and Bob's QRXs; and loss in the partial-BSM loading of Alice and Bob's emissive quantum memories.

Abstract: A multiple-access channel transmits messages from several senders to one receiver. For quantum outputs, the receiver must distinguish states depending on all messages, while tests for different decoding errors may not commute. In this paper, we give a self-contained account of simultaneous decoding, in which one joint measurement recovers the messages at the established achievable rates using independent codebooks. The proof combines Sen's tilted-subspace method with Cheng's analysis of the pretty-good measurement, a standard measurement for distinguishing quantum states. Auxiliary labels close to a common reference state separate the geometric directions associated with different errors. A Gram-matrix bound then allows separately chosen binary tests to certify the performance of one measurement. The labels are removed from the completed decoder, leading to a deterministic packing theorem that applies to finite state ensembles, including unequal priors. We develop the two-sender case first, with worked examples and figures, before treating any fixed number of senders. Sharper finite-error guarantees for the same decoder are developed separately, with sharp bounds for two specified steps of the analysis rather than a claim of globally optimal decoding. Applications recover known achievable rates for unassisted and entanglement-assisted classical communication, distributed classical compression with quantum side information, and coherent-state communication through a beamsplitter or passive multiport device. The common proof makes the geometric mechanism and its quantitative consequences available without repeating the construction for each application.

Abstract: Fault-tolerant quantum design relies on simulating noisy quantum error correction (QEC) circuits with hundreds to thousands of physical qubits and large circuit depths. High-throughput stabilizer simulators, such as Stim, are the de facto tools for this regime. However, these simulators typically support only Clifford gates and stochastic Pauli noise. As a result, more realistic device noise models-such as coherent control errors and thermal relaxation-are often approximated using Pauli Twirling. This replacement offers speed but can introduce inaccuracies.
  To address this, we propose QuasiStim for scalable exact QEC simulation of realistic noise, with QuasiFold as a compiler frontend and DeltaFrame as a sampling backend. QuasiFold lowers sampling overhead by merging equivalent non-Pauli sites in the quantum circuit. DeltaFrame samples the resulting stabilizer trajectories by reusing precomputed predicted references instead of resimulating the full tableau for each shot.
  QuasiFold can reduce the number of shots by over $100\times$ on bivariate-bicycle (BB) codes. Compared to a per-shot tableau simulator, DeltaFrame is $166\times$--$1331\times$ faster on rotated surface-code memory under coherent $R_Z$ noise as $d$ grows from 5 to 35. QuasiStim also makes the PTA bias measurable: the gap is non-negligible and varies with noise strength, decoder, and circuit schedule.
  QuasiStim will be open-sourced upon publication at https://github.com/isaachyw/QuasiStim

Abstract: Topological insulators lie outside the standard Landau paradigm: Instead of being characterized by local order parameters and spontaneous symmetry breaking, their defining properties are encoded in the global structure of their quantum states and manifested as robust boundary states. Crystalline higher-order topological insulators can extend this principle by hosting localized corner modes in two and higher dimensions. Although these boundary modes can be detected spectroscopically through the local density of states, determining the underlying bulk topology, for example through nested Wilson loops, remains an outstanding experimental challenge. Here we use up to 728 qubits in a superconducting analogue-digital quantum simulator to realize and probe a hierarchy of one-, two-, and three-dimensional topological insulators governed by effective tight-binding Hamiltonians. By reconstructing complex propagators from real-space measurements, we obtain complementary boundary and bulk characterizations: local densities of states reveal localized boundary modes, whereas experimentally reconstructed band projectors yield bulk topological invariants, culminating in a three-dimensional octupole moment extracted through third nested Wilson loops. These results establish a route to realizing, characterizing, and manipulating topologically protected states in programmable three-dimensional quantum matter.

Abstract: Authentication in quantum-aware communication settings requires mechanisms that verify user legitimacy while also assessing whether the communication channel exhibits excessive disturbance. This paper presents QIAP, a stateful hybrid framework that combines classical commitment-based challenge-response authentication with BB84-based quantum-channel monitoring. QIAP uses a dual-branch verification structure inspired by rectilinear and diagonal basis selection. In the rectilinear branch, the prover opens a hash-based commitment and the verifier checks consistency with the registered round token. In the diagonal branch, the prover returns an HMAC tag bound to the verifier nonce, round identifier, and submitted commitment without disclosing the round-specific sub-secret. Under the stated hash, HMAC, freshness, secure-registration, protected-storage, and high-entropy-secret assumptions, QIAP provides computational resistance to the modeled wrong-secret and replay attempts and protects the long-term master secret from direct recovery. After successful authentication, the BB84 module estimates the quantum bit error rate (QBER) and aborts the session when the measured value reaches or exceeds the configured threshold. In the reported simulation, no successful authentication was observed among 1,000 tested wrong-secret attempts per branch. The baseline and full intercept-resend conditions produced mean QBER values of approximately 0.02 and 0.25, respectively. The classical authentication stage required approximately 0.6 ms, and the implemented authentication messages required approximately 113 bytes per round. These results indicate that QIAP can provide a lightweight hybrid framework for combining secret-based identity verification with QBER-based channel-disturbance assessment under the stated threat model.

</details>

Abstract: Quantum teleportation, a key building block for quantum technologies, enables distant parties, Alice and Bob, to transfer an unknown quantum state using entanglement, local operations, and classical communication. Our proposal derives from a quantum theory of space-time events, whereafter we establish space as a reference for the time degree of freedom, thereby grounding our work in ongoing research on reference frames and superselection rules in quantum optics and quantum information. Beyond protocols harnessing time-frequency continuous variables of single photons, this work points toward remote synchronization in quantum communication architectures.

Abstract: The Tarski fixed-point problem asks for a fixed point of a monotone map on a $k$-dimensional grid lattice of width $n$ and has applications in game theory, economics, and verification. Substantial gaps remain between the known upper and lower bounds on its quantum query complexity.
  We prove two hardness bootstrapping theorems for quantum query lower bounds. Starting from a hard instance family whose instances have pair-wise incomparable unique fixed points, and a lower bound established by the nonnegative adversary method, we bootstrap this lower bound by (i) a factor of $Ω(t)$ when the dimension is multiplied by $t$ and the width remains unchanged, and (ii) a factor of $Ω(\log m)$ when the width is multiplied by $m$ and the dimension is doubled. Applying these theorems, we obtain three consequences: (i) an $Ω(k\log^2 n)$ lower bound for $k\geq 4$, improving previously best-known classical and quantum lower bounds; (ii) for every fixed constant $α>0$, there exists a constant $δ(α)>0$ such that $Q_ε(\text{Tarski}(n,k))=Ω(kn^{δ(α)})$ whenever $k>n^α$, and in particular, an $Ω(kn^{0.0072})$ lower bound for $k>n^{2/203}$, the first lower bound with simultaneous linear dependence on the dimension and polynomial dependence on the width; and (iii) a superpolynomial lower bound of $(k/\log k)^{Ω(\log k)}$ for exponentially wide grids, where $n=2^{Θ(k)}$. The last result rules out a route to polynomial-time algorithms for simple stochastic games and ARRIVAL through generic polynomial-query black-box Tarski solvers.

Abstract: Genuine multipartite entanglement (GME) is a valuable resource for quantum technologies, but quantifying it in many-body systems can be demanding since measures defined through optimizations over bipartitions are often assumed to require large joint measurements and many measurement settings. Our results show that the practical cost of quantifying GME can be dramatically lower than suggested by its formal definition, establishing measurement complexity as a complementary operational lens for multipartite entanglement.

This type of convertibility is characterized by the ordering of an axiomatically defined family of divergences. In this thesis, we identify this family of divergences explicitly for two scenarios, one classical and one quantum. In the classical setting, we show that the relevant divergences are certain generalizations of the bivariate Rényi divergences. Unlike previous studies, we do not require the distributions to have the same support, yielding interesting new divergences. Abstract: As quantum hardware enters the fault-tolerant regime, algorithms are beginning to be executed on encoded qubits, yet scalable certification of how well they run remains an open problem. Here we introduce magic-free certification, a certification protocol for universal fault-tolerant circuits that consumes no magic, the resource that generally places them beyond efficient classical simulation and is by far the most expensive element of a fault-tolerant computation. The protocol is code- and decoder-agnostic, runs interleaved with the target circuit on the same device, and has a sample complexity that grows linearly with the number of logical qubits and non-Clifford gates while remaining decoupled from the logical error rate itself. Given a predefined error tolerance $p_L^*$, the protocol determines at a chosen confidence level whether the device executes the target circuit at a logical error rate below $p_L^*$. The method relies on trap circuits, copies of the target with every magic state replaced by a stabilizer state, whose outcomes are deterministic and which, being indistinguishable from the target to stochastic Pauli noise, fail at the same rate. We illustrate the protocol in Steane-code simulations of a magic state injection gadget and a quantum Fourier transform.

Abstract: Quantum state texture (QST) is a recently proposed quantum resource that characterizes the nonuniformity of a density matrix in a given computational basis. In the resource theory of QST, the Fourier states f_k are maximally resourceful states, attaining the maximum QST measure. We study the QST behavior of three maximally resourceful states in a two-mode Dirac field under Hawking radiation from a Schwarzschild black hole, using a fidelity-based measure. We consider three cases: only mode B undergoes radiation, only mode A undergoes radiation, and both modes undergo radiation simultaneously. Our results show that for single-mode radiation, if the mode unaffected by radiation is orthogonal to the single-mode textureless state, the QST of the overall output state remains maximal and is independent of the Hawking temperature; we call this phenomenon orthogonality protection. When both modes undergo radiation simultaneously, orthogonality protection vanishes, and the QST of all three Fourier states decreases with increasing Hawking temperature. Notably, the QST measure of f_3 under two-mode radiation is higher than that under only mode B radiation, indicating that QST can be redistributed among modes: QST gained by one mode under radiation can partially compensate for QST lost by the other. We further provide a general resource-theoretic explanation of orthogonality protection, showing that this phenomenon is independent of the specific form of the QST measure. These results reveal how QST degrades under Hawking radiation and provide a new perspective on the redistribution of quantum resources in black hole backgrounds.

</details>

Abstract: Bell's theorem is popularly stated as the inconsistency of quantum theory with local realism. Realism is here formalized by the ontological models framework, where elements of reality are points of a measurable space and probabilities obey the Kolmogorov axioms. In the Bell scenario, locally causal ontological models posit a common cause supporting a global valuation, a joint assignment of outcomes to all measurements at once, across incompatible contexts. Within this notion of realism, Bell's theorem makes any realist explanation of the quantum predictions conspiratorial: any mechanism, whether superluminal, superdeterministic, or retrocausal, must be finely tuned so as never to manifest operationally. We redefine realism through a new ontological framework that generalizes the standard one. The elements of reality, which we call records, are no longer points but relational parts of a point-free generalization of a topological space (sublocales of a locale). A record shared by two incompatible contexts establishes the sameness of a measurement across them, without such pairwise identities composing into a global valuation, and the common cause is then a web of such records. Within this framework, Bell's no-go theorem becomes a go theorem: local causality, restated for records, is no longer at odds with the quantum statistics. This is possible because modularity, the inclusion-exclusion law of classical probability, fails across incompatible contexts, so that the probability theory generalizes Kolmogorov's. We exhibit explicit models reproducing several quantum strategies, including the optimal CHSH violation. More broadly, locally causal models of this kind attain every Bell-CHSH value up to the algebraic maximum. A realist explanation of the quantum predictions need not be conspiratorial; every causal influence posited by these models is reflected in the operational predictions.

</details>

Abstract: Since ancient Greek times, geometry has been a method by which humans can make inferences, by the use of similar triangles, about the world far beyond their immediate senses. Similarity plays an equally important role in quantum measurement, except that instead of triangles, the similarity is between the infinitesimal generators of rotation, canonical transformation, or some other virtual symmetry. For many quantum optical measurements of a single mode, these generators produce Kraus operators that are contained within a twelve-dimensional Lie group: the Instrumental Weyl-Heisenberg group manifold ($\mathrm{IWH}^{\textbf{12}}$). In particular, $\mathrm{IWH}^{\textbf{12}}$ is general enough to include all diffusively generated 'dyne measurements. In this paper, $\mathrm{IWH}^{\textbf{12}}$ will be used to develop two more general classes of 'dyne measurement: one that interpolates between diffusive homodyne and diffusive heterodyne (``slomodyne'') and one that adds to 'dyne measurements adaptive squeezing and displacement. Our analysis uses an approach known as the Instrument Manifold Program (IMP). In particular, a Harish-Chandra-like decomposition of $\mathrm{IWH}^{\textbf{12}}$ is used to calculate the vielbein transformation from a right-invariant basis to the corresponding coordinate basis, allowing us to universally and autonomously navigate $\mathrm{IWH}^{\textbf{12}}$ and locate the Kraus-operator of whatever selective process is being considered as it evolves in time according to the measurement record.

</details>

Abstract: Strong dynamical symmetries provide an algebraic description of persistent oscillations in open quantum systems governed by the Lindblad equation. For finite-dimensional systems with a full-rank stationary state, such symmetries are necessary and sufficient for non-decaying oscillatory modes to appear in the Liouvillian's spectrum. These modes can be directly constructed by acting on stationary states with the identified symmetry operators. Here we establish an asymptotic counterpart of this algebra for collective spins. Taking a single spin $S$ in a magnetic field, subject to dissipation through linear jump operators, we identify the exact conditions on the field and dissipative couplings under which non-decaying oscillatory modes emerge as $S \rightarrow \infty$. We then write down a simple, closed form for the corresponding `symmetry' operator which can be applied to the steady state to construct these modes, which are eigenvectors of the Liouvillian, with imaginary eigenvalues, up to $O(1/S)$ corrections. A second operator, an emergent conserved quantity, completes the algebra, and its slow relaxation determines how quantum noise selects the stationary state. The construction can be directly extended to a pair of interacting spins sharing a common reservoir, with the resulting quantum algebra making the entanglement during a quench analytically accessible.

Abstract: Bell-test data are typically used to certify nonlocal correlations, while quantum metrology is concerned with the sensitivity of measurement statistics to unknown parameters. Here we connect these tasks at the level of experimentally accessible Bell statistics. Abstract: We describe an optimal-control strategy designed to accelerate the dissipative preparation of complex, highly entangled quantum many-body states. Analogous to optimal steering of isolated quantum systems, our scheme identifies control pulses that guide the evolution of open quantum systems along trajectories of instantaneous steady states while minimizing leakage resulting from non-adiabatic transitions. In many cases of interest, these control parameters can be derived analytically and without the need for computationally expensive numerical simulations of the full many-body master equation. We apply this technique for the preparation of bi- and multipartite entangled states in qubit systems coupled to a common photonic waveguide or a distributed squeezed reservoir. In both scenarios, our optimal-control strategy significantly outperforms the bare relaxation dynamics after a quench, reducing preparation times of near-maximally entangled states by orders of magnitude. This drastic speedup makes dissipative entanglement distribution protocols compatible with high-fidelity quantum information processing applications and illustrates more broadly the potential of leakage minimization for accelerating reservoir-engineering schemes.

When the matching is set by the detuning, the decay costs $\approxΓ_{fe}/α$ per gate and photon, whatever the coupling strength; a deliberate mismatch or matching by a drive lowers this cost, and a qubit ancilla has no such channel. The opened decay is unheralded and flips a cat with probability $\approx 1/2$; being first order, it overtakes the second-order double decay $f\to e\to g$ as the ancilla improves. In a microscopic model of the Ocelot repetition cat code, it makes the bit-flip floor of the gates grow linearly with the cat size, by a factor 1.2-1.7 from four to ten photons, which would limit the noise bias to $\sim 10^3$. Abstract: Spatial sudden death of entanglement refers to the disappearance of quantum correlations between subsystems after a certain distance called the entanglement length. Specifically, given a density matrix $ρ_{ABC}$ on a tripartite system $ABC$ with $B$ spatially separating $A$ and $C$, the entanglement length is the distance $\ell(A,C)$ after which the reduced density matrix $ρ_{AC}$, given by tracing out the $B$ subsystem, is separable. Abstract: Scaling fault-tolerant quantum computing (FTQC) requires orchestrating millions of operations continuously across a tightly coupled classical-quantum control stack. A critical challenge in this orchestration is determining the optimal hardware-software boundary, i.e., the Quantum Instruction Set Architecture (QISA). We focus on cryogenic, fixed-connectivity qubit platforms, such as superconducting and topological Majorana qubits. For these, existing QISA choices either operate at too low a level, violating cryogenic thermal budgets at scale, or at too high a level, imposing unrealizable complexity on the control engine. To reason about these tradeoffs, we introduce the ARIES instruction set hierarchy, which categorizes QISAs into distinct abstraction levels. To systematically evaluate these levels, we develop an end-to-end evaluation toolflow featuring a cycle-accurate classical driver and hardware engine simulator. Using eight quantum applications across superconducting and topological qubit platforms, we measure instruction bandwidth, transceiver power dissipation, and measurement processing overheads. We demonstrate that while physical-level abstractions exceed the thermal limits of dilution refrigerators by orders of magnitude, canonical-level abstractions significantly underestimate hardware routing congestion and execution runtimes. Our Level 4 QISA (local logical operations) offers an optimal architectural balance. As quantum hardware platforms scale toward FTQC in the coming years, our findings provide concrete architectural guidance for the design of their classical control stacks and instruction interfaces.

Abstract: Periodically driven quantum systems exhibit a wide range of complex dynamics, including chaos. Yet, at special parameter values, their evolution can recur exactly. Several quantum protocols have recently been proposed that exploit exact Floquet recurrences to demonstrate genuine quantum advantage. Such recurrences can be studied through the arithmetic structure of the Floquet unitary. In this work, we establish a necessary and sufficient arithmetic criterion for exact recurrence and determine the minimal recurrence period when one exists. Because recurrence is a global property of the unitary, it holds for every initial state. Applying our framework requires only knowledge of the number field associated with the Hamiltonian parameters. We demonstrate the method using the kicked Ising chain, establishing exact recurrences in both Clifford and non-Clifford regimes. We further show that, in the recurrent non-Clifford regime, magic--a resource for quantum computation--is generated at intermediate times and undergoes exact periodic revivals enforced by the recurrence of the full Floquet unitary. By connecting number theory, quantum many-body dynamics, and quantum information, our work shows that exact recurrence extends beyond Clifford circuits and opens new avenues for magic generation and quantum hardware benchmarking.

Abstract: Can quantum advantages in computational efficiency coexist with quantum advantages in privacy? In this paper, we construct and use MasonQ, a universal brickwork transpiler, to answer this question with a resounding yes. We approach this question by bridging two seemingly disparate paradigms: universal blind quantum computation (UBQC), based on measurement-based quantum computation, and quantum machine learning (QML) algorithms such as the Harrow-Hassidim-Lloyd (HHL) algorithm and quantum recommendation systems.
  We provide new upper bounds on the depth of UBQC brickwork graphs, establishing that $D = \mathcal{O}(ωn)$, where $ω$ is the circuit width and $n$ its computational gate complexity. This is enabled by our newly developed transpiler MasonQ, which maps arbitrary quantum circuits to computational brickwork graph states. Given an optimal Qiskit circuit as input, we prove optimality (up to logarithmic corrections) of the MasonQ transpiler in terms of asymptotic scaling of the total brickwork graph width and depth after decomposition into bricks, enabling systematic cost analyses of blind implementations in near-term experiments and asymptotic regimes.
  Leveraging MasonQ, we derive depth bounds for several representative blind quantum machine learning (B-QML) algorithms, including the quantum Fourier transform, the HHL algorithm, recommendation systems, and quantum transformers. Lastly, we analyze concrete resource estimates for near-term demonstrations of blind quantum machine learning. We propose a minimal B-QML implementation requiring only a $12$ qubit quantum server.
  To the best of our knowledge, MasonQ is the first functional and optimal universal circuit-to-brickwork-graph transpiler to implement the full brickwork transpilation process from first principles.

Abstract: Quantum sensing is typically based on phase accumulation and is therefore fundamentally tied to the coherence time of the sensing system. Here we introduce an alternative sensing paradigm based on programmable Floquet locking in periodically driven two-level systems. We show that periodic control generates an effective Floquet locking field whose orientation and amplitude depend on global offset, allowing information to be encoded in the driven population relaxation dynamics, not in the integrated phase. We numerically demonstrate this approach for both DC and frequency-selective AC sensing, and show that temporal encoding of the response can provide enhanced precision in the presence of slow instrumental fluctuations. These results illustrate the potential of programmable Floquet responses and open the way to exploring new sensing strategies in driven quantum systems.

Allowing coherent spatial superposition maps the single-sensor problem onto Hamiltonian search and reduces the interrogation time for successfully detecting the field from $Θ(N/B)$ to $Θ(\sqrt{N}/B)$. We then extend the problem to $M$ sensors and identify a hierarchy of optimal time scalings for constant success probability determined by the available spatial and inter-sensor quantum resources. Unentangled coherently moving sensors achieve $T=Θ(\sqrt{N/M}/B)$ by searching disjoint regions in parallel, while entanglement among the sensors can further enhance the effective field coupling and achieve $T=Θ(\sqrt{N}/(MB))$. Matching lower bounds establish the optimality of these scalings in the presence of arbitrary controls and ancilla systems. Our results separate the roles of classical motion, spatial coherence, and entanglement in spatial field detection.

Abstract: Quantum random-access memory (QRAM) enables coherent queries to classical data. However, the \(O(N)\) qubits required for a memory with \(N=2^n\) addresses make QRAM appear vulnerable to noise. Though bucket-brigade routing provides intrinsic noise resilience to reduce the error in QRAM, improving the fidelity of QRAM queries is still an important and challenging problem. We introduce a low-overhead protection framework for bucket-brigade QRAM with two complementary components. First, we propose a one-fault-tolerant repeat-until-success logical controlled-SWAP gate using the \([[4,2,2]]\) code under erasure noise channel. Interleaved erasure checks and local recovery correct every single erasure, so the leading logical failure probability is second order in the physical erasure error rates. Second, we exploit validity constraints of bucket-brigade routing to define local parity checks directly on the router states, without adding extra data qubits to create code redundancy. Under bit-flip noise, minimum-weight perfect matching with noiseless syndrome measurements corrects single interior faults, while open spatial boundaries retain a first-order contribution in the residual bit-flip rate. Monte Carlo simulations confirm quadratic suppression for the protected controlled-SWAP and improved depth scaling for decoded QRAM router-state snapshots. The two codes admit a concatenated realization in which the inner code supplies erasure protection and the outer code optionally corrects interior logical bit flips. Together, these results show how routing constraints and a protected non-Clifford routing gate can improve QRAM reliability under biased and erasure-dominant noise.

Abstract: Interacting fermions owe both their non-trivial correlations and their computational power to their departure from Gaussianity. Yet, quantifying that departure geometrically faces two challenges: the Gaussian manifold is non-convex, and perturbative treatments are confined to weak interactions. Here, we introduce a geometric measure of fermionic non-Gaussianity, the fidelity distance of a target state to the manifold of fermionic Gaussian states. We construct an iteration formalism to numerically access it, applicable also at strong coupling. Further, we prove a sharpened operator Jensen inequality, of interest in its own right. We use it to derive a witness that involves only two- and four-point fermionic correlators. Numerical benchmarks on the one-dimensional Fermi--Hubbard model show that the quality of the witness improves with system size and, for strong coupling, is tighter than antiflatness. Our results open a scalable route to charting non-Gaussianity of strongly-interacting fermionic quantum simulators.

Abstract: We adapt to the context of quantum theory the treatment, familiar in relativity theory, of a reference frame as a tetrad. Since a tetrad is a set of four orthonormal vectors at a spacetime point, it encodes a timelike direction, three orthogonal spatial directions, and units; and so it gives a configuration in spacetime (a spatial orientation and state of motion) of an idealized infinitesimal apparatus (``rulers and a clock"). So a natural ``first guess" is that the state of a quantum reference frame should be an assignment of complex amplitudes to a set of tetrads. We develop this idea, in terms of bundles over spacetime of bases of the tangent spaces.
  This paper is a companion to two other papers. It emphasises the main conceptual aspects of our proposal, while the other two papers give physical details. It also explains the main contrast between those two papers. Namely: (i) the first considers a single spacetime geometry, so that a quantum reference frame, on our proposal, gives a superposition of perspectives on that geometry; while (ii) the second considers the spacetime manifold with no geometry given a priori, which will imply that a quantum reference frame, on our proposal, can describe a superposition of perspectives on a superposition of geometries---not ``just" a superposition of perspectives on a single geometry. The main mathematical idea for representing this contrast will be choosing between (i) the orthonormal frame bundle, for a given spacetime geometry; (ii) the frame bundle in the usual sense of containing all bases of all spacetime points' tangent spaces.

</details>

Lower bounds on $M_g$ follow from the performance of local algorithms: on a graph of girth $g \ge 2p+2$ a depth-$p$ local algorithm sees the same tree around every edge, so its expected cut fraction on that tree bounds $M_g$ from below. We apply this strategy to the Variational Iterative Rotation Algorithm (VIRAL), a classical local algorithm, and evaluate it on the depth-$p$ tree for every even depth up to $p=32$. Abstract: Recent efforts to detect non-Markovian memory have become increasingly important, motivated by the broad relevance of memory effects in quantum information processing, metrology, and thermodynamic engines. Here, we show that interferometric methods, particularly the phase shift and visibility, provide an experimentally accessible framework for probing environmental effects and witnessing non-Markovian dynamics. Specifically, suitably constructed witness operators enable the detection of non-Markovianity through measurable phase shifts in the interference pattern. Furthermore, we prove that the interferometric visibility is monotonically non-increasing under incoherent CP-divisible (Markovian) dynamics. Consequently, any revival of the optimized visibility constitutes a sufficient signature of information backflow and hence non-Markovianity. Our results offer a simple and experimentally feasible framework for probing memory effects, with potential applications in quantum sensing and noisy intermediate-scale quantum devices.

Abstract: Thermalization in closed quantum systems and its breakdown are central to understanding the emergence of statistical behavior in isolated many-body systems. Kinetic constraints can hinder thermalization by restricting the allowed dynamics and giving rise to non-ergodic behavior. While various aspects of ergodicity breaking in kinetically constrained models have been extensively studied in Hermitian systems, their behavior in non-Hermitian kinetically constrained models remains less explored. In particular, the inequivalence of left and right eigenstate ensembles in non-Hermitian systems raises the question of whether they exhibit qualitatively different dynamical and eigenstate properties. In this work, we study non-Hermitian generalizations of the paradigmatic PXP and PPXPP models, focusing on their Krylov complexity, eigenstate structure, and dynamics. By exploiting the Arnoldi's algorithm, we find distinct behavior of Krylov complexity in the left and right eigenstate ensembles as non-Hermiticity is increased: in the right ensemble, it exhibits persistent oscillations with an increasing period, while in the left ensemble, it gradually saturates. The characteristic arch-shaped profile of the Arnoldi coefficients is also modified differently in the two ensembles, with a Hermitian-like arch re-emerging in the left ensemble at large non Hermiticity. Additionally, in the non-Hermitian PXP model, the right ensemble retains the characteristic scar structure, while the left ensemble develops additional non-scar eigenstates with anomalously low entanglement and large overlap with the vacuum state. These findings reveal that non-Hermiticity can affect Krylov dynamics and eigenstate structure differently in the left and right ensembles, demonstrating the need to treat them separately when characterizing non-Hermitian kinetically constrained systems.

</details>

The magnetization and spin correlations show distinct features in different superposition sectors. The nearest-neighbor entanglement is largest where the transverse field and Ising interaction compete, and its behavior differs from that of the magnetic observables. We also derive the quantum Fisher information relevant to estimating the Ising interaction strength. And it exhibits multiple peaks near the critical points of the constituent ground states, with the maximum value showing Heisenberg scaling, for the parameters and system sizes examined.

Abstract: Parametrized gates in a variational quantum eigensolver (VQE) live on a product of unitary groups, which makes Riemannian optimization a natural candidate for improving training. We test this candidate in the clean per-gate setting: each single-qubit gate is optimized directly on U (2) and compared with Adam on an unconstrained Euler-angle chart representing the same physical gate, initialized from the same unitaries and tuned with a separate learning-rate sweep. On a validated hydrogen-chain benchmark, we find no measurable practical advantage from the Riemannian machinery. The direct manifold optimizer and the Euler-coordinate baseline are interleaved in final error and success rate; the classical coordinate baseline is not slower in iterations on the successful cells; and the choices of Riemannian metric, retraction, and momentum vector transport do not change the conclusion. We also identify four failure modes that can manufacture an apparent positive result: a trivial product-state benchmark, a projected-matrix Adam baseline that discards most of its own step, a non-unitary inherited Cayley retraction that lets the reported energy pass below the variational minimum, and a learning-rate grid whose optimum sits at its boundary. Each failure mode produced a plausible multi-seed result in our own workflow before being caught. The practical output of this paper is therefore a controlled null result and a validation checklist for VQE optimizer comparisons.

Abstract: Practical quantum programs interleave quantum and classical computation: classical data shape the quantum operations that are performed, and measurement outcomes shape the classical computation that follows. Formal verification of such programs in a proof assistant requires mechanizing a program logic that handles this interplay consistently at every level, from the mathematics of quantum mechanics, through program syntax and semantics, to specifications and proof rules. Such mechanizations, along with the program proofs built on them, typically require substantial manual effort.

Abstract: Quantum Machine Learning (QML) has a theoretical advantage over classical algorithms for specific synthetic data that is computationally hard. However, it remains unclear whether such theoretical advantages play a meaningful role for realistic classical data, such as natural images. Quantum structural properties induced by unitary operations or entanglement introduce inductive biases that differ from those of classical models, potentially leading to distinct learning behavior on real-world data. In a fully quantum learning pipeline, classical real-world data are first encoded into quantum states via a quantum feature mapping, analogous to a classical feature extractor, and a quantum classifier then classifies the resulting states. Quantum feature mappings generate representations that are difficult to realize classically, while also inducing representation-level biases that do not necessarily match the structure of realistic data. To enable a fair comparison at the classifier level, we focus on the classifier and use classical feature extractors that already provide powerful representations for natural images. By controlling the data scale and supervision strength, we analyze how the unitary structure of quantum classifiers affects generalization, and show that their effectiveness depends strongly on the geometry of the input representations. These results indicate that quantum classifiers benefit from classical features that are aligned with quantum feature spaces. Our analysis shows that kernel alignment identifies representations compatible with quantum feature spaces, providing insights into translating classical models into quantum algorithms.

Abstract: Variational quantum algorithms utilize parameterized circuits many times during training, at new parameter values, over batches of data, and with gradients. Pauli propagation approximates the expectation values of such circuits classically by evolving the observable in the Heisenberg picture as a truncated sum of Pauli strings. We present PADO-Pauli, a GPU engine for Pauli propagation that compiles a circuit and its observables once into sparse matrices in which each rotation angle contributes only a cosine and a sine factor, so every later evaluation reuses the compiled program. It evaluates batches of parameters and data inputs embedded as rotation angles. By default its gradients come from a vector-Jacobian product registered with PyTorch's automatic differentiation, so they flow through the angles to any classical layer that produces them. PADO-Pauli also provides truncation by Pauli weight and coefficient size, and noise channels. Zero-filtering removes terms that do not contribute to an expectation value in the all-zero reference state and makes the batched forward pass about 1.5 times faster. Compared to cuPauliProp on an NVIDIA A100, PADO-Pauli evaluates up to 10 times faster on 4 to 12 qubits and uses up to 3.1 times less memory on 16 qubits. We check its accuracy against exact statevector simulation up to 28 qubits. These features make PADO-Pauli a practical toolkit for training variational quantum algorithms, including quantum machine learning models.

These effects are minute on laboratory scales, motivating existing proposals to consider large interferometer areas to accumulate them geometrically. We quantify the experimental requirements and assess the feasibility of implementation with current or near-term technology. Our proposal thus opens a route to tabletop optical tests of the interplay between quantum mechanics and general relativity.
